Title: Square-Root Regret for Adversarial Multiplayer Bandits without Collision Information or Shared Randomness

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.05688v1

Published Time: Tue, 06 Oct 2026 02:11:12 GMT

Number of Pages: 84

Markdown Content:
# Square-Root Regret for Adversarial Multiplayer Bandits without Collision Information or Shared Randomness 

Chenyu Gan Tsinghua University 

Abstract 

We study adversarial multiplayer bandits with 𝐾 arms and 2 ≤ 𝑚 < 𝐾 labeled players, without collision information, shared randomness, or an external communication channel. We design a constructive communication and synchronization protocol with a Monte Carlo public constructor. With probability at least 1 − 𝐶𝑁 −32 over preprocessing, where 𝑁 = 2𝐾𝑚 (𝑇 + 1),its fixed published output satisfies 

𝑅 𝑇 ≤ 𝐶𝐾 5/2√𝑇 log 2 (2𝐾𝑚 (𝑇 + 1)) 

simultaneously for every oblivious reward sequence chosen after preprocessing. Here 𝑅 𝑇 is expected regret over the players’ private execution randomness. Positive reward observations establish a common learning schedule and synchronize players before learning begins. The cost of delayed communication is charged to the support of positive rewards, ensuring that periods with little useful feedback incur only limited regret. A slow–fast learning procedure then maintains valid reward estimates while assignments and scores are exchanged. 1arXiv:2610.05688v1 [cs.LG] 5 Oct 2026 Contents

21 Introduction 

In a multiplayer bandit problem, several players choose arms at the same time and see only their own rewards. If two players choose the same arm, both receive reward zero. A player who observes zero cannot tell whether the arm had zero reward or a collision occurred. Thus the same observations must be used for learning and coordination. We study the adversarial model in which the players have neither collision information nor shared randomness. We give a constructive strategy with regret 𝑂 𝑚,𝐾 (√𝑇 log 2 (𝑇 + 1)) for fixed 𝑚, 𝐾 . The players establish a common learning schedule using only their private randomness and ordinary reward observations, then learn while communicating assignment updates. 

1.1 Model and result 

Let 2 ≤ 𝑚 < 𝐾 and 𝑇 ≥ 1 be integers. Write [𝑞 ] = {1, . . . , 𝑞 } for a positive integer 𝑞 . There are 𝑚 players with distinct labels in {0, . . . , 𝑚 − 1} and 𝐾 arms. The players know 𝐾, 𝑚, 𝑇 and have a common clock. Before any private random choices, an oblivious adversary fixes rewards 𝑟 𝑡 (𝑎 ) ∈ [ 0, 1] for every 𝑡 ∈ [ 𝑇 ] and 

𝑎 ∈ [ 𝐾 ]. Player 𝑖 chooses 𝐴 𝑖,𝑡 ∈ [ 𝐾 ] and observes only 

𝑌 𝑖,𝑡 = 𝑟 𝑡 ( 𝐴 𝑖,𝑡 )1{𝐴 𝑗,𝑡 ≠ 𝐴 𝑖,𝑡 for every 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖 }. (1) Here 1{𝐸 } equals one when 𝐸 holds and zero otherwise. An action may depend on the player’s label, the time, its previous actions and observations, and its private random variables. The private randomness of different players is initially independent. There is no additional communication channel and no initially common random seed. The constructive model also permits public preprocessing: a Monte Carlo constructor samples finite tables depending only on 𝐾, 𝑚, 𝑇 , then publishes all of them before the adversary chooses the reward matrix. No preprocessing randomness remains secret. The execution randomness of the players is independent of preprocessing and initially independent across players. Regret below is conditional on a fixed published output. Fixing a successful output instead gives deterministic tables in the original model. For 𝑥 ∈ R𝐾 and 𝑆 ⊆ [ 𝐾 ], put 𝑥 (𝑆 ) = Í𝑎 ∈𝑆 𝑥 𝑎 , and let S = {𝑆 ⊆ [ 𝐾 ] : |𝑆 | = 𝑚 }. Identify 𝑟 𝑡 with its vector of arm rewards. Expected regret is

Here 𝑁 is a convenient size parameter and Λ its logarithm. Constants denoted by 𝑐, 𝐶 are positive and universal, and their values may change between appearances. The notation 𝑥 ≲ 𝑦 means 𝑥 ≤ 𝐶𝑦 , and 𝑥 ≍ 𝑦 

means both inequalities. In particular, no dependence on 𝐾, 𝑚, 𝑇 is hidden in these constants. 

Theorem 1 (Constructive regret bound) . For every 2 ≤ 𝑚 < 𝐾 and 𝑇 ≥ 1, there is a Monte Carlo public constructor and a finite-arithmetic private-randomness strategy in model (1) . With probability at least 

1 − 𝐶𝑁 −32 over preprocessing, its fixed published output satisfies, simultaneously for all oblivious reward matrices, 

𝑅 𝑇 ≲ 𝐾 5/2√𝑇 Λ2. (3) 3The adversary may select its matrix after inspecting that output. The public description has 𝑂 (𝑁 4 log 𝑁 )

bits and setup takes e𝑂 (𝑁 4) bit operations. Total online bootstrap decoding uses e𝑂 (𝑁 5/2) bit operations; the remaining learner also has polynomial bit complexity. 

For 𝑚 = 𝐾 , player 𝑖 always choosing arm 𝑖 + 1 gives zero regret. The case 𝑚 = 1 is the ordinary adversarial bandit problem and requires no communication. 

Comparison with the previous bound. For oblivious adversarial multiplayer bandits with neither collision information nor shared randomness, the only previously known general sublinear upper bound is that of Bubeck et al. [BLPS20, Theorem 21]: e𝑂 (𝑚𝐾 3/2𝑇 1−1/( 2𝑚 ) ). For fixed 𝑚, 𝐾 , our bound improves the exponent of 𝑇 from 1 − 1/( 2𝑚 ) to 1 /2, matching the standard Ω(√𝑇 ) adversarial-bandit lower bound up to logarithmic factors. In particular, for 𝑚 = 2 and fixed 𝐾 ≥ 3, we obtain 𝑂 𝐾 (√𝑇 log 2 (𝑇 + 1)) = 𝑜 (𝑇 2/3). This disproves the first conjecture in [BLPS20, Section 2.5] that the two-player regret in this setting is Ω(𝑇 2/3). Theorem 1 has a successful public output for each 𝐾, 𝑚, 𝑇 ; fixing it before rewards are chosen gives deterministic tables visible to the adversary, while the players use only independent private randomness during execution. 

1.2 Positive support and regret 

A positive reward proves that the observed arm had no collision: 

𝑌 𝑖,𝑡 > 0 =⇒ 𝐴 𝑗,𝑡 ≠ 𝐴 𝑖,𝑡 ( 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖 ). (4) The communication protocol uses this implication to reject incorrect messages. Its duration may be large in calendar time. To measure the relevant delay, define the support at time 𝑡 and its cumulative value by 

𝑔 𝑡 =

> 𝐾

∑︁ 

> 𝑎 =1

1{𝑟 𝑡 (𝑎 ) > 0}, 𝐺 (𝑡 ) =

> 𝑡

∑︁ 

> 𝑢 =1

𝑔 𝑢 . (5) Since 𝑟 𝑡 (𝑆 ) ≤ 𝑔 𝑡 , the comparator earns at most the positive support on any interval. We therefore charge communication delay to increments of 𝐺 . After rewards are rounded, the same notation refers to the rounded rewards. 

1.3 Why communication is possible in this model 

There are two information problems. First, the receiver cannot identify a collision from a zero reward. The usual binary protocol “collide for zero, avoid for one” is therefore not a reliable bit channel: the second action also produces zero whenever the underlying reward is zero. In particular, an all-zero reward interval carries no observable message. We assume neither a positive lower bound on rewards nor a bound on the length of an all-zero interval. Positive observations still give one-sided information. If a receiver plays arm 𝑎 and receives a positive reward, every candidate message that would have caused a collision on 𝑎 is false. A zero observation rejects no candidate. Repeated positive observations can therefore remove false messages. The arm arrays also ensure that the actions of the other players do not remove all such observations. Second, the players initially have no common random row for arm choices, codes, recording decisions, or assignment updates. The protocol first communicates the root player’s private row index by means of deterministic public arrays and independent private column choices. Only then does it use the selected learning row. 41.4 Organization. 

Section 3 outlines the protocol and analysis. Section 4 establishes a shared public row and bounds the cost of initialization. Section 5 develops the slow–fast learner and proves its regret bound. Sections 6 and 7 specify communication, local updates, and scheduling, and bound the cost of delayed messages and interruptions. Section 8 combines these bounds and transfers the product-stream analysis to the published table. Section 9 gives finite samplers and computational bounds. Appendix A analyzes exceptional executions; Appendix B constructs the public table and proves its uniform and bootstrap communication guarantees. 

Acknowledgement of AI use. The author used OpenAI’s GPT-6-astra to assist in developing the public-table construction and protocol design, and OpenAI’s GPT-6-sol to assist in drafting and revising proofs. The author verified the correctness and originality of all content, including references. 

# 2 Related work 

Stochastic rewards. Anantharam, Varaiya, and Walrand [AVW87] studied the stochastic bandit problem with multiple plays under centralized control. For decentralized players whose simultaneous choices can collide, Liu and Zhao [LZ10] established logarithmic regret bounds; subsequent algorithms include the musical-chairs approach of Rosenski, Shamir, and Szlak [RSS16]. Bubeck and Budzinski [BB20] gave an 

𝑂 (√︁ 𝑇 log 𝑇 ) regret strategy for two players and three arms with shared randomness and no communication that avoids collisions with high probability. Bubeck, Budzinski, and Sellke [BBS21] extended the high-probability collision-free guarantee and e𝑂 (√𝑇 ) regret to arbitrary numbers of players and arms, again using shared randomness. Bonnefoi et al. [BBM +18] introduced the setting in which players observe rewards but cannot distinguish a collision from a zero reward, and Besson and Kaufmann [BK18] also studied this feedback model. Lugosi and Mehrabian [LM22] gave the first regret guarantees without collision information or shared randomness: an instance-dependent logarithmic bound and, when a positive lower bound on the mean of the 𝑚 -th best arm is known, a gap-free square-root-type bound. Boursier and Perchet [BP19] obtained an instance-dependent bound in the no-collision-information setting by communicating through deliberate collisions, assuming a known positive lower bound on every arm mean. Shi, Xiong, Shen, and Yang [SXSY20] used error-correcting codes for this communication. Huang, Combes, and Trinh [HCT22] gave an instance-dependent algorithm that does not require a lower bound on the smallest arm mean. 

Adversarial rewards. Alatur, Levy, and Krause [ALK20] gave the first decentralized adversarial multiplayer bandit algorithm with sublinear regret, obtaining a e𝑂 (𝑇 2/3) bound with collision information. Bubeck, Li, Peres, and Sellke [BLPS20] obtained a e𝑂 (√𝑇 ) bound for two players with collision information. Without collision information or shared randomness, they proved e𝑂 (𝐾𝑇 3/4) regret for two players and 

e𝑂 (𝑚𝐾 3/2𝑇 1−1/( 2𝑚 ) ) for 𝑚 players, against an oblivious adversary. They also showed that an adaptive adversary can force linear regret without shared randomness. Shi and Shen [SS21] studied the no-collision-information model through an attackability parameter and proved sublinear regret under conditions on that parameter using coded collision communication. 

# 3 The algorithm in brief 

The players must learn which arms reward them while keeping their choices collision-free. Following the slow–fast approach of Bubeck et al. [BLPS20], one player acts as a coordinator and can change arms 5frequently; the other 𝑚 − 1 players keep assignments long enough to communicate observations. The rule for choosing the coordinator’s arm is deliberately biased: choosing uniformly can make a promising arm hard to observe once the other arms have been assigned. Section 5 gives the rule and its regret analysis. Messages can take several rounds. The players therefore record a small sample of observations in a way that has the same learning law during ordinary play, assignment delivery, and score collection. A player can save an observation before learning its assignment and decide later whether it belongs in the sample. If a new assignment is proposed while scores are being collected, the next assignment must account for that proposal; simply restarting from the current learning weights would lose information about why collection began. The recording and communication rules appear in Sections 6 and 7. The delay costs regret only when positive rewards were available. This lets the proof charge communication to the number of arms with positive rewards rather than to calendar time. The main theorem combines this charge with the learner’s regret bound in Section 8. The players begin without shared random bits. Before play, a constructor publishes a table of independent control rows. The coordinator privately selects one row and communicates its index during initialization. On a successful initialization all players use that row; failures are paid for in expected regret. The analysis first studies an independent random row and then shows that a suitable fixed published table works for every oblivious reward sequence chosen after publication. The initialization and public-table arguments are in Section 4 and Appendix B. Figure 1 shows the physical workflow. 6Once, before play. Algorithm 10 publishes the table. Initialization has two purposes: establish a common table row and ensure that every follower is active before the first learning assignment. This section bounds its cost by positive reward support; the bootstrap communication rules are in Appendix B. We use the per-round support 𝑔 𝑡 and cumulative support 𝐺 (𝑡 )

defined in (5). The public table has 𝑀 = 𝑁 2 rows. For row 𝑗 , write 𝐶 𝑗,𝑡 for its reserved-round indicator at time 𝑡 and 𝑅 𝑗 

for its independent learning/control symbols. Let 𝐶 𝐴 be a sufficiently large universal constant, whose required size is fixed in the proof of Lemma 29, and set 𝐴 key := ⌈𝐶 𝐴 𝑁 ⌉. This is the authentication-key alphabet size. After publication, each follower draws an independent key 𝑈 𝑖 ∼ Unif ([ 𝐴 key ]) , and the root independently draws 𝐽 ∼ Unif ([ 𝑀 ]) . The selected learning/control row is 𝑅 𝐽 .The uniform draws in the algorithms below and in Appendix B specify the exact distributions used in the proofs. Their finite implementation uses the following bounded sampler. Set 

𝑎 cap := ⌈45 log 2 𝑁 ⌉. (6) For a set 𝑆 of size 𝑞 > 1, BUnif (𝑆 ) makes at most 𝑎 cap independent attempts. In each attempt it draws 

⌈log 2 𝑞 ⌉ fair bits, interprets them as 𝑋 ∈ { 0, . . . , 2⌈log 2 𝑞 ⌉ − 1}, and accepts the (𝑋 + 1)-th element of 𝑆 if 

𝑋 < 𝑞 . For 𝑞 = 1 it returns the sole element without drawing bits. If all attempts are rejected, it reports 

exhaustion ; a private call returns the first element of 𝑆 , whereas the public constructor publishes the fixed-arm fallback flag and stops. All calls use fresh independent bits. The root index and follower keys, as well as the uniform choices during bootstrap, are implemented by these calls. Each call is coupled to its exact-uniform counterpart by the same rejection stream; their outputs agree unless the cap is exhausted. The finite public constructor obtains a Bernoulli variable with rational parameter 𝑟 /𝑠 by testing whether BUnif ([ 𝑠 ]) ≤ 𝑟 ; it generates permutations by bounded Fisher–Yates exchanges [Dur64].

Pathwise support and independence. Put 𝐻 r = 8𝑒 (𝑐 act + 1/2)𝑄 boot . Through 𝜏 , only bootstrap actions occur, so 𝜏 and the bootstrap history through 𝜏 are Fboot -measurable. The reference correction, when needed, and subsequent bootstrap updates use only this information. Induction over blocks thus makes every reference bootstrap state and activation time Fboot -measurable. The correction precedes the first control-row access, and every such access uses 𝑅 𝐽 . □

For analysis on exceptional paths, use the following bounded continuation after the first weight-doubling update. Keep the logical scores fixed, set all subsequent logical estimates to zero, and perform no further logical refreshes. Whenever a later auxiliary reward is evaluated, use 𝑈 𝑡 = {1, . . . , 𝑚 − 1} and 𝑗 𝑡 = 𝑚 . This convention affects only paths with a stability failure and keeps each auxiliary reward in [0, 𝑚 ]; a decoder failure alone does not stop or alter the logical learner.

5.3 Learning bound 

For an episode starting at 𝜏 , let H𝜏 be the full history immediately before its initial slow-set draw. Its next grid boundary 𝑔 = min {𝑇 + 1, 𝐻 (⌊ 𝜏 /𝐻 ⌋ + 1)} and recording rates are fixed under this conditioning. Define the smaller history 

E𝑡 = 𝜎  H𝜏 , e𝑟 𝜏 , . . . , e𝑟 𝑡 −1

, 𝜏 ≤ 𝑡 < 𝑔. 

Thus E𝑡 records the episode’s initial history and subsequent estimates, and omits subsequent proposals and communication observations. The scores and 𝑝 𝑡 are E𝑡 -measurable. 

Lemma 6 (Conditional slow-set law) . For each round 𝑡 of the episode, 

Pr (𝑈 𝑡 = 𝑈 | E 𝑡 ) = 𝑝 𝑡 (𝑈 ) on {𝑡 ≤ 𝜏 ∗}.

The event that no previous weight doubling has occurred is E𝑡 -measurable. The identity therefore applies on the weight-doubling round itself. 

18 Proof. The initial draw gives the identity at 𝑡 = 𝜏 . Suppose it holds at 𝑡 and that no earlier update doubled a weight. The law in (21), followed by the deterministic formula (22), defines a probability kernel 𝐾 𝑡 for e𝑟 𝑡 .This kernel depends only on the frozen rates, the fixed reward vector, and whether the round is enabled. All these quantities are E𝑡 -measurable. In particular, for every set 𝐶 of possible estimates, Pr (e𝑟 𝑡 ∈ 𝐶 | F 𝑡 ) = 𝐾 𝑡 (𝐶 ).

For each slow set 𝑈 , the tower property now gives Pr (𝑈 𝑡 = 𝑈, e𝑟 𝑡 ∈ 𝐶 | E 𝑡 ) = E[1{𝑈 𝑡 = 𝑈 }𝐾 𝑡 (𝐶 ) | E 𝑡 ]

= 𝑝 𝑡 (𝑈 )𝐾 𝑡 (𝐶 ).

Consequently revealing the estimate leaves the conditional law of 𝑈 𝑡 equal to 𝑝 𝑡 . On an enabled nonreserved round, the proposal bit is independent of the history through that estimate. Its refresh draw has law 𝜌 𝑡 and is independent of the preceding slow set. Therefore Pr (𝑈 𝑡 +1 = 𝑈 | E 𝑡 +1) = (1 − ℎ) 𝑝 𝑡 (𝑈 ) + ℎ𝜌 𝑡 (𝑈 )

= 𝑝 𝑡 +1 (𝑈 ).

On a reserved round the estimate is zero and neither distribution changes. This proves the induction until the first doubling. Whether an earlier update doubled a weight is determined by the estimates already included in 

E𝑡 . Restricting to this event therefore does not add information about 𝑈 𝑡 beyond E𝑡 , which proves the last assertion. □

# 6 Assignment and score communication 

Use the history F𝑡 of Section 5 after any commitment at the start of round 𝑡 and before its physical arm choices. It includes the resulting assignment and all previously used coordinates; it excludes the current slot, pilots, arm tuple, hash, fast-arm draw, thinning coordinate, and all still-unread proposal coordinates. Thus the local states and outgoing words are F𝑡 -measurable. In the product-stream execution the current physical coordinates have their prescribed independent laws conditional on F𝑡 . Write 𝑌 𝑖,𝑡 for the quantized observation, using the convention for 𝑟 𝑡 in Section 5. The slot 𝜏 𝑡 on a nonreserved round has the following law. Slot Probability Role DATA 1/2 Play arms and possibly record one reward. DOWN 1/4 Send an assignment and confirm that its recipient decoded it. UP 1/4 Return a score, or certify that the coordinator received all assignment receipts. 22 6.1 Message decoding and support cost 

On each DOWN or UP round, the row selects 𝑘 𝑡 ∈ [ 𝑑 ] uniformly and supplies an independent uniform injective arm tuple (𝑅 0,𝑡 , . . . , 𝑅 𝑑,𝑡 ) ∈ [ 𝐾 ]𝑑 +1. Conditional on 𝑘 𝑡 = 𝑖 , DOWN goes from the coordinator to player 𝑖 ; UP goes from player 𝑖 to the coordinator. Write 𝑅 𝑗 when the round is clear. Every integer field uses ⌈log 2 (𝑇 𝐷 + 1)⌉ bits, where 𝐷 is the score quantization denominator of Section 5. A DOWN record has four fields; an UP record has three fields followed by a zero field. Both have length 

𝐵 = 4⌈log 2 (𝑇 𝐷 + 1)⌉ . Section 7.2 gives the encoding and field bounds. For each round and each direction and selected player, independently draw 𝑐 𝑡 ∈ F𝐵  

> 2

and 𝑏 𝑡 ∈ F2, and define

23 Lemma 8 (Message decoding) . Consider the product-stream protocol with correct initialization, stopped before a stability or feasibility failure. With probability at least 1 − 𝛿 /𝑁 4, no completed batch has a unique solution different from the word currently encoded on its channel that passes (40) , and no completed batch formed entirely from one fixed awaited word has multiple solutions. On this event, an awaited word is accepted within 2𝑄 dec positive receiver observations after its sender fixes that word, provided service continues. Each decoder decision takes 𝑂 (𝑄 dec 𝐵 2) = 𝑂 (log 3 𝑁 ) bit operations. 

Appendix A.2 proves this lemma. A decoder failure is a wrong acceptance or a nonunique system in a complete batch whose observations all come from the fixed awaited word. A decoder failure contributes at most 𝑚𝑇 𝛿 /𝑁 4 to expected regret. The next lemma bounds the cumulative charge of parallel tasks when each pending task’s conditional service probability is at least a fixed multiple of the round’s charge and each task has a service quota. 6.3 Recording rewards and delayed local sums 

During an assignment, follower 𝑖 owns arm 𝑎 𝑖 and the coordinator owns 𝐹 . During collection the coordinator owns all arms. Ownership specifies whose observation can supply an arm’s increment. Lemma 19 (Assignment identities and fixed replies) . In the reference execution, every follower applies each assignment at most once. Each assignment has one cutoff and one ENDPOINT per follower. After its cutoff is known, the derived UP word stays fixed until the next DOWN word is applied. During a known live assignment no UP message is encoded. No later assignment is committed before all current ENDPOINTs have been accepted. When a known live follower becomes unoccupied, its DOWN batch is empty. At each assignment’s first cutoff, every coordinator UP batch is empty. An interruption that leaves a follower unoccupied preserves its existing partial DOWN batch. Proof. Scores and live assignments. Initially both executions have zero scores. Suppose that at a physical commitment the two score vectors agree and the physical slow set is coupled to the logical slow set. On every subsequent round on which recording is enabled, Lemma 14 gives the physical recorded arm and increment the same conditional law as the estimate step of Algorithm 3. We therefore use the same (𝐼 𝑟 , Δ𝑟 )

41 in both executions. On rounds on which recording is disabled, set Δ𝑟 = 0 in both. Hence, since both score vectors receive the same increments, 

𝑍 (𝑟 ) = b𝑧 (𝑟 )

until the first stability failure. While the assignment is live, use the same proposal bit in the two executions. When the physical coordinator draws a fast arm, use that same draw for the logical learner; otherwise draw the logical fast arm independently from its prescribed conditional law. Thus the two slow sets agree throughout the live assignment, up to their common cutoff 𝑢 .

Subsequent coordinates are drawn freshly. The coupling may correlate this vector with the auxiliary logical randomness, which imposes no restriction on the physical protocol’s marginal law. Induction over commitments now gives 𝑍 (𝑟 ) = b𝑧 (𝑟 ) before the first stability failure and 𝑈 phys (𝑟 ) = b𝑈 𝑟 on every live round before that failure, completing the proof. □

7.6 Score collection and total cost of interruptions 

In the product-stream reference execution, fix the rounded reward matrix and reserved mask. Let H contain nonreserved rounds from that commitment through the last DOWN receipt, or through the round before the next cutoff if a receipt remains missing. Finally, 𝑝𝑇 ≤ √𝑇 and 1 /𝑝 ≤ 4 + 2√𝑇 yield 𝑄 boot = 𝑂 (𝑉 ) and 𝑚 𝑝𝑇 + 𝑚𝐾 Λ = 𝑂 (𝑉 ).Substitution in (59) proves 𝑅 𝑇 (𝑟 ) = 𝑂 (𝑉 ) for every rounded matrix on the fixed public output. For an arbitrary original reward matrix 𝑟 orig , rounding down changes each comparator reward by at most 

𝑚 /𝐷 per round, whereas the actual unrounded reward is at least the rounded reward. Consequently, 

𝑅 𝑇 (𝑟 orig ) ≤ 𝑅 𝑇 (𝑟 ) + 𝑚𝑇 𝐷 = 𝑂 (𝑉 ).

All bounds hold simultaneously for every rounded matrix on the same output in Gpub , and the rounding comparison is pathwise. This proves the required quantifier over all oblivious reward matrices. On the complementary parameter branch use a fixed assignment to 𝑚 distinct arms. For two 𝑚 -sets 𝑆, 𝑆 ′,

|𝑆 \ 𝑆 ′ | ≤ 𝑘 ★, so 𝑟 𝑡 (𝑆 ) − 𝑟 𝑡 (𝑆 ′) ≤ 𝑘 ★. The resulting regret is at most 𝑘 ★𝑇 ≤ 𝐶 𝑏 b𝑉 = 𝑂 (𝑉 ). This proves (3) for every horizon. The computational bounds follow from Section 9 below. □

# 9 Finite computation and sampling 

We bound the computations performed by the preceding protocol. At each commitment, Algorithm 7 draws an assignment using Algorithm 9 below: the initial and post-grid draws use one product distribution, and a within-episode draw after collection uses three signed product terms. The two-term refresh in Algorithm 3 belongs to the logical execution; the physical coordinator implements its accumulated effect at commitment. Algorithm 10 constructs the finite public rows used for these draws and for control. During play, Algorithm 12 calls the bootstrap decoder Algorithm 11 at complete block boundaries, whereas Algorithm 8 calls the control decoder Algorithm 4 on enabled DOWN and UP channels. 

Arithmetic model. We use a random-access bit-cost model with numerical inputs 𝐾, 𝑚, 𝑇 . For a reward observation 𝑌 ∈ [ 0, 1], the feedback interface supplies ⌊𝐷𝑌 ⌋, including 𝐷 when 𝑌 = 1. Thus processing an arbitrary real reward requires only its prescribed quantization. All subsequent operations use integers, rational numbers, and random bits.

9.2 Storage and bit complexity 

Proposition 26 (Finite implementation complexity) . On the construction branch, the public description has 𝑂 (𝑁 4 log 𝑁 ) bits and can be constructed using e𝑂 (𝑁 4) bit operations. The total bootstrap-decoding cost is e𝑂 (𝑁 5/2), and the total post-initialization communication-decoding cost is 𝑂 (𝑚𝑇 log 3 𝑁 ) on every execution. The latter improves to 𝑂 (𝑇 log 3 𝑁 ) when all active players use the same row. All remaining online computations have polynomial bit complexity in 𝐾, 𝑚, 𝑇 .Moreover, the bounded integer-sampling implementation is total, and the probability that any capped constructor or private-bootstrap draw reaches its cap is at most 𝑁 −40 .Proof. We use the parameter estimates established in the proof of Lemma 29: 

𝑚𝐵 + = 𝑂 (√𝑁 ), 𝑆 col = 𝑂 (𝑁 3/2), 𝑄 arr = 𝑂 (𝑁 2),

together with 

𝑚𝑇 ≤ 𝑁, 𝐾 2𝑇 ≤ 𝑁 2,

and the fact that every arm, message, column, and sampler coordinate has 𝑂 (log 𝑁 ) bits. 

Public construction. The raw arm arrays contain 

𝑂 (𝑚𝑇 𝑆 col )

entries, while the stored message permutations contain 

𝑂 (𝑚𝐵 +𝑆 col 𝑄 arr ) = 𝑂 (𝑁 4)

labels. The reserved masks use 𝑀𝑇 ≤ 𝑁 3 bits. By Lemma 25, the finite learning/control rows contain 𝑂 (𝐾 2)

𝑂 (log 𝑁 )-bit coordinates per row and time, and hence use 

𝑂 (𝑀𝑇 𝐾 2 log 𝑁 ) = 𝑂 (𝑁 4 log 𝑁 )

54 bits. The certified code is only polylogarithmic in 𝑁 . Thus the entire published description has 𝑂 (𝑁 4 log 𝑁 )

bits. The same counts show that the constructor performs at most 𝑂 (𝑁 4) finite sampling, permutation, and table-entry operations. Each such operation involves 𝑂 (log 𝑁 )-bit quantities and bounded rejection sampling with 𝑂 (log 𝑁 ) attempts. Hence the total construction cost is e𝑂 (𝑁 4) bit operations. 

Decoding. At a complete bootstrap block boundary, checking one sender column requires a pass over at most 𝑛 positions and Gaussian elimination in 𝑂 (log 𝑁 ) binary unknowns. Its cost is therefore 

𝑂 (𝑛 log 2 𝑁 ).

Each receiver has at most three neighboring senders, so the number of sender-column checks over all blocks is 𝑂 (𝑚𝐵 +𝑆 col ). Since 𝐵 +𝑛 ≤ 2𝑇 ,

𝑂 (𝑚𝐵 +𝑆 col 𝑛 log 2 𝑁 ) = 𝑂 (𝑚𝑇 𝑆 col log 2 𝑁 )

= e𝑂 (𝑁 5/2).

For the post-initialization affine channels, 

𝐵, 𝑄 dec = 𝑂 (log 𝑁 ).

A full decoder decision solves at most 𝑄 dec equations in 𝐵 binary unknowns and hence costs 𝑂 (log 3 𝑁 ) bit operations. Each player makes at most one channel decision on a calendar round, so the unconditional total cost is 

𝑂 (𝑚𝑇 log 3 𝑁 ).

When all active players use the same row, its common slot and selected follower enable at most one receiver channel per round. This gives 𝑂 (𝑇 log 3 𝑁 ) for synchronized executions, including the reference. 

Remaining online computation. It remains only to check that exact learner and scheduling arithmetic uses polynomial-size operands and polynomially many operations. The quantization denominator satisfies 𝐷 = 𝑂 (𝑁 2) and every score lies in [0, 𝑇 𝐷 ]. Hence an exact weight 

𝑤 𝑖 =

 𝐷 + 1

𝐷 

 𝑧 𝑖 

has numerator and denominator of polynomial bit length. Products, elementary-symmetric-sum dynamic programs, and the at most three rational components used by the commitment sampler therefore also have polynomial bit length. The proposal probability ℎ has 𝑂 (log 𝑁 ) bits, while 

𝜅 = (1 − ℎ)𝑛 𝑢,𝑡 +1

ℎ , 𝑛 𝑢,𝑡 ≤ 𝑇, 

has polynomial bit length as well. All message fields have 𝑂 (log 𝑁 ) bits. There are at most 𝑇 commitments and 𝑂 (𝑚𝑇 ) local round updates. The loops appearing in the learner, recorder, scheduler, and direct sampler have lengths polynomial in 𝐾 and 𝑚 , and the sparse observation lists contain at most 𝑇 records per follower. Consequently all remaining online computations use polynomially many arithmetic, comparison, hashing, and list operations on polynomial-bit operands. Their total bit complexity is therefore polynomial in 𝐾, 𝑚, 𝑇 .55 Finally, the constructor and private bootstrap together make 𝑂 (𝑁 4) bounded uniform-integer draws, and hence at most 𝑁 5 such draws after enlarging the fixed branch constant if necessary. A single rejection attempt succeeds with probability at least 1 /2, so with 

𝑎 cap = ⌈45 log 2 𝑁 ⌉

a particular call reaches its cap with probability at most 2 −𝑎 cap . Therefore Pr {some bounded sampler reaches its cap } ≤ 𝑁 52−𝑎 cap ≤ 𝑁 −40 .

The prescribed fallback rules define the execution even on this event, so the finite implementation is total. □

56 A Exceptional executions 

The decoder estimates apply through the first weight doubling. A decoder failure does not terminate or condition the logical learner in the reference execution. 

A.1 A total local algorithm 

The following convention defines every action even after an erroneous declaration or decode. It uses one absorbing error state, denoted †, for each player. Before performing a local operation, check that its inputs belong to their stated finite domains. In particular, arm labels must belong to [𝐾 ], scores to Z ∩ [ 0, 𝑇 𝐷 ],increments to Z ∩ [ 0, 𝐷 ], and time fields to [0, 𝑇 + 1]. Every required denominator must be positive. Each Bernoulli parameter, including a thinning or mixture parameter, must belong to [0, 1]. For the slow-set sampler, apply these checks to the scalar probabilities and normalizers that its successive sampling steps actually evaluate (Section 9.1). The local record must specify exactly one applicable action branch. Acomputed value is checked before it is stored or used in another operation. Let Φ be one specified local operation, with input domain dom (Φ) and allowed output set DΦ. Apply its total extension 

Φ(𝑥 ) =

(

Φ(𝑥 ), 𝑥 ≠ †, 𝑥 ∈ dom (Φ), Φ(𝑥 ) ∈ D Φ,

†, otherwise . (67) The first branch evaluates the operation only after its input-domain checks. Once the state is †, no learning or transport update is performed. A decoder rejection is an ordinary operation and does not enter †. In the error state the player plays arm 

𝑖 + 1 on active nonreserved rounds and continues its bootstrap-array action on declared reserved rounds. Since 0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑑 = 𝑚 − 1 < 𝐾 , this arm always exists. Bootstrap updates and activation retain their own specified finite-domain convention.

> amb

it is the unique solution and passes (40). Thus, if service continues for (𝑄 − 𝑛 ) + 𝑄 ≤ 2𝑄 further positive observations, 𝑀 ∗ is accepted. Finally, solving 𝑄 binary equations in 𝐵 unknowns costs 𝑂 (𝑄𝐵 2) = 𝑂 (log 3 𝑁 ) bit operations by Gaussian elimination; parsing costs 𝑂 (𝐵 ). This proves all assertions. □

# B Public construction and bootstrap communication 

This appendix specifies the public table and proves its simultaneous guarantees. The mask and arm-array properties hold for all reward supports and actual bootstrap messages; the finite-row bound holds for all rounded reward matrices. The constructor samples once and does not test these universal properties of its output. As throughout the paper, 𝑐, 𝐶 > 0 denote universal constants whose values may change between appearances. All probability arguments in this appendix use the exact sampling laws displayed in the algorithms. The bounded implementation is compared to those laws by the rejection-stream coupling of Section 4, with cap exhaustion charged separately. 

B.1 Public construction and reserved masks 

Recall from Section 4 that 𝑀 = 𝑁 2 is the number of published learning/control rows. Recall also the construction branch (11), the fixed concentration constant 𝐶 0, the reserved-round probability 𝑝 , the bootstrap block length 𝑛 , and the finite-coordinate precision 𝐿 bit = ⌈64 log 2 𝑁 ⌉. These are precisely the parameters needed by the public constructor below. Algorithm 10 samples the masks, certified code, bootstrap arrays, and finite learning/control rows in that order, using fresh randomness at each stage. 

Algorithm 10 Construct and publish the public table 

Require: 𝐾, 𝑚, 𝑇 .1: if 𝑘 ★𝑇 ≤ 𝐶 𝑏 b𝑉 then 

2: Publish the fixed-arm fallback flag; stop. 3: end if 

4: Sample 𝐶 𝑗,𝑡 ∼ Bernoulli ( 𝑝 ) independently for 𝑗 ∈ [ 𝑀 ] and 𝑡 ∈ [ 𝑇 ].5: Using randomness independent of the masks, construct the certified code 𝐺 of Subsection B.3. 6: if code certification fails then 

7: Publish the fixed-arm fallback flag; stop. 8: end if 

9: Sample the label permutations and raw arm-array coordinates of Subsections B.2 and B.3. 10: Independently sample 𝑀 finite learning/control rows as defined in Subsection B.4, using 𝐿 bit bits per scalar coordinate and independent fair affine-hash bits. 11: Publish all masks, the certified code 𝐺 , every label permutation 𝜋 𝑠,𝑏,𝑤 , all raw arm-array coordinates, and all learning/control rows. On successful construction, the published table has the following components. The fixed-mask condition in Lemma 29 is used when proving its reserved-position property A7. Algorithm 10 specifies exact sampling laws. B.5 Bootstrap decoding and outward protocol 

Fix a block 𝑏 and a receiver 𝑖 . Let 𝑤 𝑖 be the column chosen by 𝑖 for this block, and abbreviate L𝑖 := L𝑖,𝑏,𝑤 𝑖 

and 𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡 ) := 𝑏 𝑖,𝑏,𝑤 𝑖 (𝑡 ). Let 𝐴 𝑖,𝑏 ⊆ 𝐼 + 

> 𝑏

be the positions at which 𝑖 follows its bootstrap array action. Its positive listening set is 

𝑃 𝑖,𝑏 := {𝑡 ∈ L 𝑖 ∩ 𝐴 𝑖,𝑏 : 𝑌 𝑖,𝑡 > 0},

where 𝑌 𝑖,𝑡 is its observed reward. Before activation, 𝐴 𝑖,𝑏 = 𝐼 + 

> 𝑏

; after activation, it consists of the declared reserved positions. Write 𝑃 = 𝑃 𝑖,𝑏 when 𝑖, 𝑏 are fixed, and call |𝑃 | ≥ 𝑒 ∗ the positive-observation quota. The receiver knows the labels of its parent and children from the fixed tree, but a positive observation carries no sender label. B.7 List bounds, authentication and recovery 

The results of this subsection are stated for the all-bootstrap auxiliary execution with exact-uniform private draws. In Proposition 3 we couple this execution to the physical initialization. Since the two executions agree until the first activation, and the support bound below implies that recovery occurs before any activation on the good event, the recovery guarantees transfer to the physical initialization. Recall that ℎexc is the allowed exceptional-position count and 𝜖 arr is the exceptional-column fraction. For the fixed support and receiver listening choices used above, recall 

𝐸 = {𝑡 ∈ L 𝑖 : 𝜒 𝑡 (𝑏 𝑖 (𝑡 )) = 1}, 𝑠 = |𝐸 |,

and, for a tuple 𝑥 of the other senders’ raw entries, 𝑃 𝑥 ⊆ 𝐸 is the resulting positive listening set defined in (88).

Let a row be bad for 𝜒 if either inequality fails at some complete-block boundary. There are at most 𝑇 such boundaries, so its probability is at most 2 𝑇 𝑒 −𝐶 0Λ ≤ 𝑁 −30 . The bad-row indicators are independent across 𝑗 .As 𝑀 = 𝑁 2,Pr {more than 𝑀 /𝑁 rows fail } ≤ ( 𝑒𝑁 −29 )𝑁 .

There are 2 𝐾𝑇 support matrices and 𝐾𝑇 ≤ 𝑁 /4. Consequently the probability that (ii) fails is at most exp {𝐾𝑇 log 2 + 𝑁 (1 − 29 log 𝑁 )} ≤ 𝑁 −40 .

For (i), apply the upper exponential-moment bound with every weight equal to one, first on [𝑇 ] and then on each complete block. Each count exceeds twice its mean plus 𝐶 0Λ with probability at most 𝑒 −𝐶 0Λ. The union over at most 𝑀 (𝑇 + 1) counts is at most 𝑁 −40 after increasing 𝐶 0. This proves both claims. □
